## Registry And Reflection Relationship

The ECS registries and the reflection registry are deliberately separate layers:

- `ComponentRegistry` assigns runtime `ComponentId` values for typed Rust components and dynamic plugin component presence. Its identity surface is folder-backed under `component/{mod,marker,id,registry}.rs`: `marker.rs` owns the `Component` trait and default storage type, `id.rs` owns the typed id, and `registry.rs` owns descriptor allocation plus Rust/dynamic lookup.
- `ResourceRegistry` assigns runtime `ResourceId` values for typed scene resources. It is the system-param/resource-store identity layer.
- `ComponentTypeRegistry` remains the scene-side JSON descriptor cache for dynamic components. Plugins submit the neutral `core/framework/scene::ComponentTypeDescriptor`; the plugin facade no longer owns or re-exports that DTO.
- `TypeRegistry` is the schema/read/write reflection authority. It stores neutral `ReflectTypeRegistration` metadata plus optional component/resource adapter slots used by `WorldReflection`.

Dynamic plugin component registration now feeds both paths in one mutation sequence: validate/project the `ComponentTypeDescriptor` into a reflected JSON schema, reject duplicate reflected type paths before mutating descriptor state, register the descriptor in `ComponentTypeRegistry`, then insert the dynamic `ReflectComponent` adapter into `TypeRegistry`. This lets ECS storage/query code keep stable component IDs while editor inspectors, resources, remote DTO tests, and future scene serialization use one reflected schema surface.

`WorldReflection` is not a storage owner. It resolves type paths through `TypeRegistry`, routes component addresses to `ReflectComponent`, routes resource addresses to `ReflectResource`, and calls normal `World` mutation/read APIs so change ticks, dynamic JSON property validation, fixed component dirty bits, and resource ticks stay coherent with the ECS layer.

## World Integration

`World` owns `EntityRegistry`, `ComponentRegistry`, `ComponentStorage`, `ResourceRegistry`, `ResourceStore`, `CommandQueue`, and the current `ChangeTick` as skipped runtime state. Spawning, inserting saved node records, despawning, and project loading update or rebuild those runtime-only maps while serialized scene files remain unchanged. This keeps current scene roundtrips stable and gives later milestones live internal identity/component/resource maps for archetype movement, queries, deferred commands, and change detection.

Project scene assets now persist the full M5 fixed light set that the world can author: ambient, directional, point, rect, and spot lights. `World::from_scene_asset(...)` converts `SceneAmbientLightAsset` and `SceneRectLightAsset` into the same fixed component maps used by editor-created nodes, and `World::to_scene_asset(...)` writes them back so save/load does not lose the Bevy-aligned ambient and rectangular area light product fields.

Direct `World` serde deserialization follows the same runtime-only rehydration rule without invoking full project-load normalization. The custom deserialize path restores the persistent scene maps, then rebuilds `EntityRegistry` and typed component presence so `contains_component_id(...)` observes serialized fixed/dynamic scene components immediately, without adding default cameras/lights or other project policy defaults.

The M2 typed API is intentionally narrow:

- `World::spawn(bundle)` creates a stable scene entity and inserts a `Bundle`.
- `World::insert`, `World::get`, `World::get_mut`, and `World::remove` work for Rust `Component` types.
- `World::component_id`, `World::registered_component_id`, `World::registered_dynamic_component_id`, `World::component_count_for_id`, `World::contains_component_id`, and `World::contains_component<T>` expose component identity/presence for tests and later query work.
- `World::is_component_added<T>` and `World::is_component_changed<T>` expose direct component tracker checks over typed ECS storage. They use the same `World::last_change_tick()` / `World::read_change_tick()` observation window as the direct resource tracker API, while system params and query filters keep their own per-system run windows through `SystemState`.
- Existing fixed scene setters such as rigid body, transform, active state, render-layer mask, and dynamic plugin component attachment/property writes now flow through the typed component presence path instead of maintaining a separate identity model.
- Fixed scene component maps still back serialization, property paths, editor projections, and render extract data. Typed storage mirrors these values so M3+ queries can converge without breaking the existing public scene model. The light map set now includes Bevy-informed ambient and rect light authoring data beside directional, point, and spot lights; rect light direction is transform-derived during render extraction rather than stored as a separate field. The 2026-06-08 M5 property-read lookup follow-up stopped allocating normalized candidate strings while scanning fixed property entries; the latest 2026-06-11 read path now passes the requested component and property segment slices directly and compares both sides through `normalized_identifier_matches(...)` without allocating a normalized target segment vector. Fixed reads still route through `property_entry_value(...)` and the shared `visit_property_entries(...)` visitor, so `World::property(...)` can stop on the first matching fixed literal path without building a full `ScenePropertyEntry` vector or formatting dynamic-component entry paths before the dynamic fallback. The matching property-entry projection follow-up still pre-sizes returned `ScenePropertyEntry` storage from fixed component presence, mesh morph weights, animation parameters, collider/joint optional fields, and projectable dynamic JSON scalars before pushing entries in the same public order; dynamic component capacity counting walks JSON property values directly instead of building an iterator filter/count chain, and the hierarchy parent entry is pushed directly after the initial missing-entity guard instead of rechecking entity membership. The entity-path resolution follow-up borrows `EntityPath::segments()` once and walks each candidate ancestry through `entity_matches_path_segments(...)`, preserving duplicate sibling labels from `path_segment_for_entity(...)` without allocating a candidate `EntityPath` vector and raw path string for every scanned entity. Duplicate sibling labels now use `entity_has_duplicate_path_name(...)` with an early-exit sibling search rather than counting the full same-name sibling set.

## M12 Fixed Storage Cutover Map

M12 is not a blanket deletion of every fixed scene map. The Bevy-parity target is to remove duplicate behavior paths, while preserving the fixed data that still owns Zircon's serialized scene product. The current ownership split is:

- Fixed `World` maps remain authoritative for persistent scene product fields: stable node records, direct serde/project load state, scene asset import/export, editor hierarchy rows, property-path compatibility, unified derived/special reflection adapters for built-in components, and render extract product data.
- Skipped derived-state maps remain runtime caches owned by scene systems: `world_matrices`, `active_in_hierarchy`, and `node_cache` are rebuilt from hierarchy/transform/active inputs rather than serialized as independent truth.
- Typed ECS storage owns runtime component identity, component presence, change ticks, query access, archetype/cache metadata, system params, observers, messages, events, resources, and schedule conflict detection. New system-facing behavior should enter through these typed APIs instead of adding another direct fixed-map read/write path.
- Reflection owns editor/remote field routing. `WorldReflection` calls normal `World` mutation/read APIs and must not become a separate storage owner.

The first M12 prep cutover follows this split: plain entity-id query-cache helpers and test-only introspection methods no longer widen the runtime lib surface. The remaining fixed maps should be removed only after their serialization/editor/render extract responsibility has a concrete replacement. Current search hits that are not deletion candidates yet include `DynamicScene::from_versioned_json(...)` loading old `ProjectDocument { world }` payloads as a one-way migration boundary, `WorldReflection` facade naming as the intended DTO router, and render-layer `legacy_mask` helpers owned by the render/asset compatibility path rather than the ECS kernel.

## Bevy-Parity Examples

The M12 example owner is [bevy_parity_examples.md](bevy_parity_examples.md). It maps the supported Zircon API shape back to Bevy source anchors for `World`, `Query`, `Commands`, `Changed<T>`, observers, reflection, scene, and transform propagation. The examples intentionally show typed ECS for runtime behavior, fixed maps for still-owned serialized product fields, and `WorldReflection` as a DTO router rather than another storage layer.

## Resources And Events

`ResourceStore` and `EventStore` are typed support layers for scene systems. `ResourceStore` exposes typed `insert`, `get`, `get_mut`, and `remove` operations over `TypeId` ownership, while `EventStore` now assigns dense `EventTypeId` slots during registration and keeps typed event queues in a slot array. Resource eligibility has one neutral declaration owner: `core/framework/scene/resource.rs` defines `SceneResource`, allowing lower framework DTOs and plugins to declare resource values without importing the concrete scene domain. The scene-facing `Resource` vocabulary resolves to that same declaration through the explicit leaf owner `scene/ecs/resource/marker.rs`; the marker module contains only the controlled vocabulary export and does not duplicate the trait. `scene/ecs/resource/mod.rs` remains a structural route, `id.rs` owns typed `ResourceId`, and `registry.rs` owns `ResourceDescriptor` plus `ResourceRegistry` TypeId allocation and descriptor lookup. Runtime 08's resource-store owner remains folder-backed under `scene/ecs/resource_store/`: `stored_resource.rs` owns erased boxed values, type names, and resource `ComponentTicks`, while `store.rs` owns the `TypeId` registry and typed operations. Runtime 08's event owner is folder-backed under `scene/ecs/events/`: `id.rs` owns event ids, `metrics.rs` owns payload/capacity DTOs, `queue.rs` owns `Events<T>`, `cursor.rs` owns reader cursors, `subscription.rs` owns dormant/connected reader state, and `store.rs` owns erased channel registry and dense slot lookup. `EventStore::register::<T>()` returns a stable dense slot, and `EventReaderParam<T>` / `EventWriterParam<T>` cache that slot during `SystemParam::init_state(...)` so runtime reads and writes can use slot lookup instead of repeating a `TypeId` map lookup. `ResourceStore` records `ComponentTicks` for resources too, allowing direct `World` resource inspection and `Res<T>` / `ResMut<T>` system params to expose `is_added()`, `is_changed()`, and `last_changed()`. `Events<T>` keeps a `next` queue and a `current` queue; `send` publishes into the next frame, `send_batch` publishes each input event into that same next queue and returns the number written, while `update` swaps it into the readable queue. The explicit `Default` implementation intentionally does not require `T: Default`. Event queues now also carry allocation policy metadata: the next-frame write queue is preallocated from the observed high-water length, retained storage is shrunk only after `EVENT_CAPACITY_SHRINK_DEBOUNCE_FRAMES` consecutive low-water updates, and `EventCapacityMetrics` exposes the current/next lengths, capacities, high-water mark, debounce counter, and shrink count for diagnostics.

The 2026-06-19 Runtime 05 support-first repair keeps event channel registration and reader activation as separate concepts. A registered dense `EventTypeId` is enough for `EventStore::send_by_id(...)` and `send_batch_by_id(...)` to append to the `next` queue, even while `reader_count == 0`; an unknown event type id still returns `false` or `0`, and type-id/type mismatches keep the existing assertion path. `EventStore::is_active(...)` now only reports whether an active reader/subscription is connected. `EventWriterParam<T>` initialization registers the event type, so writer-before-reader systems can queue events and a later `EventReaderParam<T>` observes them after `World::update_events::<T>()`. `EventStore::update_all()` remains wired as the built-in `zircon.scene.events_update_all` system in `SystemStage::First`, so all registered event channels advance from `next` to `current` at one schedule point instead of each event type needing a manual update call. `EventSubscription<T>` is the weak-dependency reader primitive: it registers the event type while dormant, increments the reader count only when connected, and clears the cursor to the current queue position on connect so plugin activation never replays historical events that were queued before activation. `EventPayloadProfile` records the payload size of every dense event channel; `EVENT_INLINE_PAYLOAD_MAX_BYTES` is fixed at 128 bytes, and payloads larger than that are classified as `IndirectRecommended`, meaning plugin contracts should wrap the large data behind `Arc` or another stable handle instead of sending the full object inline. This deliberately follows Bevy's cursor and double-buffer semantics for frame-delayed events while adding Zircon's connection-state diagnostics, capacity debounce, and payload diagnostics for plugin-facing event channels.

`World::resource_id`, `World::registered_resource_id`, `World::contains_resource`, `World::insert_resource`, `World::resource`, `World::get_resource`, `World::resource_mut`, `World::get_resource_mut`, and `World::remove_resource` expose the M2 resource API over `ResourceRegistry` and `ResourceStore`. `World::resource` and `World::resource_mut` intentionally panic on missing resources; use the `get_*` variants for optional access. `World::is_resource_added` and `World::is_resource_changed` mirror Bevy's direct world resource tracker checks against `World::last_change_tick()` and `World::read_change_tick()`, while `World::clear_trackers()` advances the direct-observation baseline shared by direct component/resource checks without changing per-system `SystemState` run windows.

`World::send_event`, `World::update_events`, `World::clear_events`, `World::events`, `World::event_type_id`, `World::event_reader_count`, `World::event_payload_profile`, `World::event_capacity_metrics`, `World::register_dormant_event_subscription`, `World::connect_event_subscription`, `World::disconnect_event_subscription`, and `World::read_event_subscription` expose the runtime event queue and its dense registration state. `EventWriterParam<T>` writes single events or counted batches through `EventStore::send_by_id` / `send_batch_by_id` into `next`, while `EventReaderParam<T>` owns a per-system `EventCursor<T>` and cached `EventTypeId` so `iter(&mut self)` reads only unread entries from the current queue and advances that cursor. Zircon events do not yet assign per-event ids, so batch event writes return only the count of accepted events; cursor messages remain the id-bearing scheduled communication path. The queue generation advances on `World::update_events`, `World::clear_events`, and `Events::clear`, letting the same reader observe the next frame even when the new current queue is shorter than the previous one. Runtime 08-M3.1 locks the split: events stay hidden in `next` until explicit update, `clear_events` prunes both current and next event queues, and event update/clear has no effect on `Messages<T>`. This mirrors Bevy's current/next event visibility rule and remains separate from the M6 immediate observer and cursor-message paths below.

Runtime 05 additionally pins the writer-before-reader repair with `event_store_send_by_id_uses_registered_channel_guard_source`: send paths must keep checking registered-channel presence and must not reintroduce `EventStore::is_active(...)` or `reader_count` as producer-write gates. `first_stage_event_update_all_uses_builtin_source_path` keeps the matching update path explicit by requiring the built-in `zircon.scene.events_update_all` descriptor to stay in `SystemStage::First`, dispatch through `InternalSceneSystem::UpdateEvents`, and call `World::update_all_events()` into `EventStore::update_all()`. `event_subscription_source_keeps_dormant_reader_boundaries` locks the dormant subscription split by requiring `new_dormant(...)` to use `register::<T>()` instead of reader registration, `connect(...)` / `disconnect(...)` to be the only reader-count transition points, and disconnected reads to clear the cursor and return `EventReadIter::empty()`.

## Observers, Lifecycle, And Messages

M6 adds the first Bevy-style push observer and scheduled message foundations without replacing the older `Events<T>` queue. The split is intentional:

- `World::trigger_event(event)` and `World::trigger_entity_event(entity, event)` are immediate observer paths. Registered callbacks run during the trigger call, before control returns to the caller.
- `World::send_message(message)` writes to a retained `Messages<T>` buffer. `MessageReaderParam<T>` owns a persistent `MessageCursor<T>` so a system only reads messages it has not consumed before.
- Existing `Events<T>` remains the current/next frame queue used by earlier system-param tests and plugin-adjacent runtime flows.

`ObserverStore` is runtime-only skipped world state. It stores cloneable function-table callbacks for three scopes:

- component lifecycle observers keyed by `LifecycleEventKind` plus `ComponentId`;
- global immediate event observers keyed by Rust `TypeId`;
- entity-targeted immediate event observers keyed by Rust `TypeId` plus stable `EntityId`.

Observer callbacks are cloned out of the store before invocation. This avoids borrowing `World::observers` while user code receives `&mut World`, and allows observers to mutate the world through the normal typed API. The clone-out path counts matching callbacks first and builds an exact-capacity callback vector for lifecycle, global event, and entity-targeted event triggers. `ObserverId` is an opaque runtime handle allocated from one counter across all observer scopes; `World::remove_observer` now searches lifecycle, global event, then entity-targeted observer lists with a first-match helper and returns as soon as the matching observer is removed. This preserves callback order inside each list while avoiding full retain scans across lists that cannot contain the same id. Runtime 08-M2 fixes the timing verdict: lifecycle observers fire synchronously during component mutation, entity-event observers only receive their target entity, and removing an observer during dispatch affects later triggers without skipping or double-firing callbacks already cloned for the active dispatch. Message retention now keeps the newest generation window within explicit entry, declared-byte, and age budgets; a slow reader observes its truncated sequence through `dropped_count`, clear does not reset `MessageId` allocation, and clear generation boundaries are not reported as reader lag.

`LifecycleEventKind` currently covers `Add`, `Insert`, `Replace`, `Remove`, and `Despawn`, matching the milestone language while staying close to Bevy's lifecycle split between add/insert and removal/despawn behavior. `World::insert<T>` triggers `Add` for first presence, `Replace` for an existing component, and `Insert` after every successful insert. `World::remove<T>` triggers `Remove` before the component leaves storage. `World::remove_entity` triggers `Remove` and `Despawn` for every component id still present on that entity before storage and fixed scene maps are cleared.

Removed-component readers are the scheduled pull side of lifecycle removals. `RemovedComponentReader<T>` keeps a per-system cursor into `RemovedComponentEvents`, advances to the current event tail on each read, and now sizes its returned `Vec<EntityId>` from the unread slice before pushing entities directly. The public `RemovedComponentsParam<T>` API still returns an iterator over that vector, so the M5 change removes implicit collection growth without changing system-param call sites.

`MessageStore` is the scheduled counterpart to immediate observers. It stores `Messages<T>` by `TypeId`, assigns monotonically increasing `MessageId<T>` values, and exposes typed `MessageWriterParam<T>` / `MessageReaderParam<T>` system params. Runtime 08's message owner is folder-backed under `scene/ecs/messages/`: `id.rs` owns `Message`, its retained-byte charge, and `MessageId<T>`; `queue.rs` owns the bounded `Messages<T>` log plus retention telemetry; `cursor.rs` owns sequence-window `MessageCursor<T>` and `MessageReadIter`; and `store.rs` owns erased registration, typed read/write lookup, and frame advancement over its single active-retention-channel worklist. Message reads do not clear the shared log; the reader cursor advances per system state and reports budget/age truncation. `World::configure_message_retention<T>` selects entry, byte, and age ceilings, `World::message_retention_metrics<T>` exposes retained/drop counts, `World::last_message_advance_channel_visits()` exposes the current First-stage worklist visit count, First-stage `UpdateEvents` advances only active retention channels, and `World::clear_messages<T>` remains the explicit generation boundary.

## Schedule Convergence Guardrails

The current scene schedule consumes the unique `core/framework/scene::SystemStage` contract and is converged on `First -> PreUpdate -> FixedFirst -> FixedUpdate -> FixedPostUpdate -> Update -> PostUpdate -> Last -> RenderExtract`. The concrete scheduler, registry, barriers, and World mutation remain scene-owned; the retired scene-local stage declaration was deleted rather than left as a duplicate or compatibility module. M01 therefore treats schedule behavior as verification rather than another stage migration. The focused M01 tests and source audit prove:

- built-in derived systems remain registered in `PostUpdate`, with render-extract preparation in `RenderExtract`;
- native systems, plugin hooks, internal descriptors, and `ApplyDeferred` barriers share one deterministic sorted stage list;
- command-producing native systems expose an `ApplyDeferred` barrier in both scheduled steps and conflict-graph batches;
- duplicate/blank system ids and missing required resources report structured initialization diagnostics;
- the runner's post-step built-in flush is success-only (`result.is_ok()`), while existing success-path tests keep current dirty-flag built-ins idempotent;
- scene scheduling owner paths (`src/scene/ecs`, `src/scene/module`, and `src/scene/world`) must not contain `LateUpdate`, which prevents local compatibility aliases, shims, reintroduced stages, and bridge exports.

`SceneScheduleRunner` still executes current internal built-ins through the sorted step list and then calls `run_internal_scene_systems_for_stage(stage)` again only when the stage succeeds. Current built-ins are dirty-flag idempotent, so that double-flush is safe today and useful as a final consistency sweep. Future non-idempotent internal scene systems should not be added to this path until the runner contract is narrowed or the system is made explicitly idempotent.

## Derived-State Dirty Lanes

M02 keeps the derived scene systems inside the existing schedule vocabulary instead of adding a new stage. `DerivedStateDirty` still tracks hierarchy, active hierarchy, transforms, node cache, and render-extract freshness. The dependency lanes are:

- hierarchy mutations mark hierarchy, active, transforms, node cache, and render extract;
- active-self mutations mark active, node cache, and render extract;
- transform mutations mark transforms, node cache, and render extract;
- node-cache-only product-field mutations mark node cache and render extract.

`RenderExtractPrepare` remains the render-extract freshness boundary: it runs hierarchy validity, active hierarchy, world transform, and node-cache systems before the render-extract dirty flag is cleared. The systems are still idempotent rebuilds from authoritative maps, so repeated `PostUpdate` or `RenderExtract` flushes must not duplicate node records, corrupt parent links, or change results after the first successful flush. If a future internal scene system needs non-idempotent side effects or exact-once execution, it must not enter this path until `SceneScheduleRunner`'s success-final-flush contract is narrowed with Technical Advisor approval.

Projected read APIs intentionally do not clear dirty flags. `local_transform(...)` reads the authoritative local-transform component directly; `active_in_hierarchy(...)`, `world_transform(...)`, `world_matrix(...)`, `node_records()`, and `find_node(...)` can reflect pending hierarchy/active/transform inputs for editor/runtime reads, while `nodes()` remains the retained cache and only refreshes after `PostUpdate`/`RenderExtractPrepare`. Hot callers that need only a transform use `local_transform(...)` rather than the owned full-record projection of `find_node(...)`. `set_active_camera(...)` and property-path component edits that affect render rows mark node-cache/render-extract freshness only on real changes; no-op setters keep the world clean. M02 validation lives in `zircon_runtime/src/scene/tests/derived_state.rs` and the workflow evidence file listed in this document header.

## Query Model

The M3 query foundation adds `QueryState<D, F>` under `zircon_runtime::scene::ecs::query` and exposes it through `World::query<D>()` and `World::query_filtered<D, F>()`. Query state is a compiled access descriptor rather than an owning view of the world: it records the component reads, writes, `With<T>` filters, and `Without<T>` filters needed by a system or manual caller, while `World` remains the authority for entity storage and component values.

Query data marker types are intentionally allowed to be borrowed shapes such as `&T`, `&mut T`, and `Option<&T>` rather than `'static` marker structs. `Component` itself remains `'static + Send + Sync`; the query marker only describes access, while the fetched item lifetime comes from the `World` borrow passed to `QueryState::iter(...)` or `QueryState::for_each_mut(...)`.

The initial supported data forms are deliberately narrow:

- `&T` reads a required `Component`.
- `&mut T` mutably reads a required `Component` through mutable helpers, while immutable query helpers project it as `&T`.
- `Ref<T>` reads a required component with `is_added()`, `is_changed()`, and `last_changed()` metadata.
- `Mut<T>` mutably reads a required component with the same metadata surface as `Ref<T>` and uses the existing mutable fetch path that marks the component changed; immutable query helpers project it as `Ref<T>` so tick metadata can be inspected without marking the component changed.
- `Option<&T>` reads an optional component without filtering out entities that lack it.
- `EntityId` returns the stable public scene entity id.
- `StableEntityLocation` returns the internal stable-to-generational location cached by `World`.
- tuples up to eight items compose query data/filter shapes in the current hand-written implementation ceiling.

`With<T>` and `Without<T>` compose as query filters and also participate in access disjointness. `Added<T>` and `Changed<T>` read component tick metadata against the current system run window; `Changed<T>` includes newly added components, matching Bevy's behavior. Tick filters record their component through `QueryAccess::add_filter_read(...)` rather than the normal data-read path, so `QueryState<&mut T, Changed<T>>` and `QueryState<&mut T, Added<T>>` can read tick metadata for their own mutable data item without tripping same-query alias checks. Those filter reads still remain in `QueryAccess::reads()` so cross-query scheduling and conflict diagnostics continue to see that the query observes the component. `QueryAccess::conflicts_with` reports a conflict when two states both touch the same component and at least one writes it, unless their filter sets prove the matching entity sets are disjoint, such as `With<Player>` versus `Without<Player>`. `QueryAccess::conflicting_components_with(...)` returns the concrete `ComponentId` set for the schedule-conflict layer. `QueryState::try_new` rejects conflicting data access inside one query, including duplicate `&mut T` access, while allowing the query's own tick filters to coexist with that mutable item.

This is Bevy-inspired but intentionally smaller than Bevy's full `WorldQuery` system. Mutable tuple query data is still deferred; the current mutable path supports one mutable data item (`&mut T` or `Mut<T>`) so M3/M5 can establish borrow-safety diagnostics and change-tick behavior before wider system execution.

M11 starts the performance-parity path by adding explicit query cache metadata without changing the default query API. Bevy's `QueryState` stores matched table/archetype bitsets plus an archetype generation and updates them when the world gains new archetypes; Bevy's query iterators then set a table or archetype storage context before fetching item rows. Zircon's first M11 slice added an entity-set cache: `QueryState::update_cache(world)` stores stable entity ids that structurally match the query data and archetype membership, and `QueryState::iter_cached(world)` iterates that cached set while still fetching current component values and evaluating filters such as `Added<T>` and `Changed<T>` against the active tick window. Dynamic tick filters are intentionally rechecked during iteration rather than baked into the cached entity list. `World` owns a runtime-only query cache revision that advances on entity spawn/despawn and component presence add/remove, but not on value replacement. This means component replacement can reuse the cached entity list while still reading fresh component values from the world. The revision is ignored for `World` equality because it is runtime execution metadata, not scene data.

The second M11 slice adds the first Bevy-style archetype metadata beneath that cache. `World` now maintains an `ArchetypeIndex` keyed by `ArchetypeSignature`, and structural component insert/remove moves each live entity to the signature matching its table/sparse component set. `QueryAccess` now separates component reads/writes from required `With` membership, so `Option<&T>` participates in borrow access without falsely requiring `T` during archetype candidate selection. `QueryState::update_cache(world)` records the matched archetype ids and archetype generation before collecting candidate entity locations in scene order. It caches stable entity ids, `StableEntityLocation` records, and per-entity `ComponentStorageLocation` records for the accessed read/write components that are present. Cache rebuild now evaluates `QueryDataAccess::matches_component_locations(...)` against those locations before accepting a candidate entity, so required data (`&T`, `&mut T`, `Ref<T>`, and `Mut<T>`) can be checked from storage metadata while optional/entity/unit data stays non-structural. This gives later dense-iteration work internal entity ids plus component table rows/sparse-set addresses without changing query results.

The cached-direct M11 slice connects those storage locations to an explicit read-only iteration path. `CachedQueryData` is implemented for the current read-only item shapes (`EntityId`, `StableEntityLocation`, `&T`, `Ref<T>`, `Option<&T>`, `()`, and tuples up to eight items), and `QueryState::iter_cached_direct(world)` returns `CachedQueryIter`. This iterator uses cached `StableEntityLocation` and `ComponentStorageLocation` entries plus the stale-safe `ComponentStorage::get_with_ticks_at_location(...)` helper, while still rechecking filters and required data before yielding an item. `StableEntityLocation` is Zircon's Bevy-inspired counterpart to Bevy's `EntityLocation` query data in `bevy_ecs/src/query/fetch.rs`: the normal query path resolves it from `World`, while cached-direct iteration returns the already cached location from `QueryState`. `CachedQueryData::matches_cached_data(...)` defaults to the same `QueryDataAccess::matches_component_locations(...)` predicate used by cache rebuilds; concrete direct-fetch implementations still use location reads for `&T`, `Ref<T>`, optional `&T`, and the read-only projections of mutable data (`&mut T` as `&T`, `Mut<T>` as `Ref<T>`). `CachedQueryFilter` keeps filter rechecks on the same cached-location path for the built-in filters: structural `With<T>` / `Without<T>` rely on the cache's archetype candidate set, while `Added<T>` / `Changed<T>` read ticks from cached component locations. `CachedQueryManyIter` extends the same direct-location path to caller-provided read-only entity lists. Actual mutable query data still fetches through the existing `fetch_mut` path, but `QueryState::for_each_mut(...)` and system `Query::for_each_mut(...)` now refresh and consume the persistent cached structural candidate entity list before rechecking dynamic filters, so mutable system queries no longer start from a full-world entity scan.

The tuple-arity slice expands `QueryData`, `QueryFilter`, `CachedQueryData`, and `CachedQueryFilter` tuple implementations from four to eight elements. Bevy uses `all_tuples!` to implement query data and query filters from 0 to 15 elements in `bevy_ecs/src/query/fetch.rs` and `bevy_ecs/src/query/filter.rs`; Zircon deliberately stops at eight in this slice to match the local system-param tuple ceiling while leaving derive-style query data/filter as the later Bevy-parity answer for wider shapes.

The read-only single-result slice adds `QuerySingleError::{NoEntities, MultipleEntities}` plus `QueryState::single(...)`, `QueryState::single_cached(...)`, `QueryState::single_cached_direct(...)`, `Query::single(...)`, and cached system variants. The implementation follows Bevy's `Query::single` / `single_inner` shape in `dev/bevy/crates/bevy_ecs/src/system/query.rs` and Bevy's `QuerySingleError` location in `dev/bevy/crates/bevy_ecs/src/query/error.rs`: Zircon peeks at the first two yielded items and succeeds only when exactly one entity matches. Zircon intentionally keeps the error variants payload-free for now because the local query API does not yet carry Bevy's debug query type names.

The mutable single-result slice adds `QueryState::single_mut(...)` and system `Query::single_mut(...)` for the current single-item `QueryMutData` surface. This follows Bevy's mutable `single_mut` API in `dev/bevy/crates/bevy_ecs/src/query/state.rs` and `dev/bevy/crates/bevy_ecs/src/system/query.rs`, while adapting to Zircon's explicit cached mutable query path: the implementation refreshes the structural candidate cache, scans for exactly one entity that still passes required data and dynamic filters in the active tick window, and only then fetches the mutable item. This preserves the same `QuerySingleError::NoEntities` / `MultipleEntities` behavior as read-only `single(...)` without ever holding more than one mutable query item while testing uniqueness.

The targeted-get slice adds `QueryEntityError::{NotSpawned, QueryDoesNotMatch}` plus read-only `QueryState::get(...)`, `QueryState::get_cached(...)`, `QueryState::get_cached_direct(...)`, `Query::get(...)`, and cached system variants. This follows Bevy's `Query::get(entity)` and `QueryState::get(...)` shape in `dev/bevy/crates/bevy_ecs/src/system/query.rs` / `dev/bevy/crates/bevy_ecs/src/query/state.rs`, and Bevy's `QueryEntityError` location in `dev/bevy/crates/bevy_ecs/src/query/error.rs`. Zircon keeps the first mismatch shape intentionally small: `NotSpawned(entity)` means the stable scene id is not live, while `QueryDoesNotMatch(entity)` means the entity exists but lacks required data or is filtered out by `With<T>`, `Without<T>`, `Added<T>`, or `Changed<T>`. Later mutable and unique-many slices extend the enum with `AliasedMutability(entity)` and `DuplicateEntity(entity)` for stricter alias/uniqueness preconditions.

The many-get slice adds read-only `QueryState::get_many(...)`, `QueryState::get_many_cached(...)`, `QueryState::get_many_cached_direct(...)`, `Query::get_many(...)`, `Query::get_many_cached(...)`, and `Query::get_many_cached_direct(...)`. It follows Bevy's array-returning `QueryState::get_many` in `dev/bevy/crates/bevy_ecs/src/query/state.rs` and system `Query::get_many` / `get_many_inner` in `dev/bevy/crates/bevy_ecs/src/system/query.rs`: the output order matches the input entity array, and read-only duplicate entities are allowed because no mutable alias can be created. Zircon's cached variants refresh the persistent candidate list once and then fetch every requested entity against the same tick window, so `Changed<T>` / `Added<T>` filters stay consistent across the array.

The unique many-get slice adds read-only `QueryState::get_many_unique(...)`, `QueryState::get_many_unique_cached(...)`, `QueryState::get_many_unique_cached_direct(...)`, `Query::get_many_unique(...)`, `Query::get_many_unique_cached(...)`, and `Query::get_many_unique_cached_direct(...)`. Bevy's equivalent read-only `get_many_unique` accepts `UniqueEntityArray<N>` in `dev/bevy/crates/bevy_ecs/src/query/state.rs` and delegates to system `Query::get_many_unique_inner(...)` in `dev/bevy/crates/bevy_ecs/src/system/query.rs`; the uniqueness contract itself is represented by `EntitySet`, `EntitySetIterator`, and `UniqueEntityIter` in `dev/bevy/crates/bevy_ecs/src/entity/entity_set.rs`. Zircon now uses the same typed unique input for normal, cached, and cached-direct read-only many-get helpers. Duplicate stable entity ids are rejected when constructing `UniqueEntityArray<N>`, while successful fetches still preserve array order, still return `NotSpawned` / `QueryDoesNotMatch` for live-world mismatches, and still use one tick window across each request.

The read-only `iter_many` slice adds `QueryManyIter`, `QueryState::iter_many(...)`, and system `Query::iter_many(...)`. It follows Bevy's `Query::iter_many` / `QueryState::iter_many` APIs in `dev/bevy/crates/bevy_ecs/src/system/query.rs` and `dev/bevy/crates/bevy_ecs/src/query/state.rs`, with the core Bevy contract preserved: requested entities are visited in caller-provided order, duplicate read-only entity IDs can yield duplicate items, and missing or non-matching entities are skipped instead of reported as errors. `QueryEntityItem` accepts both owned `EntityId` values and borrowed `&EntityId` items, so callers can pass arrays, vectors, or borrowed entity lists without allocating an intermediate array.

The cached read-only `iter_many` slice adds `QueryState::iter_many_cached(...)` and system `Query::iter_many_cached(...)`. This is Zircon's explicit counterpart to Bevy's `QueryState::iter_many_manual(...)` shape: it refreshes the local structural cache once, filters the caller's entity list through `cached_entities`, then uses the same `QueryManyIter` fetch path to recheck dynamic filters such as `Added<T>` and `Changed<T>` against the active tick window. The resulting iterator still preserves input order and duplicate read-only IDs, but skips entities absent from the cached structural candidate set before fetching values. Iterator unique-entity specializations remain a later slice because they need the same unique entity-set reasoning as Bevy's `iter_many_unique` families.

The cached-direct read-only `iter_many` slice adds `QueryState::iter_many_cached_direct(...)` and system `Query::iter_many_cached_direct(...)`. It uses the same caller-order and duplicate read-only semantics as `iter_many(...)`, but resolves the requested entity list to cached candidate indices once and then fetches each item through `CachedQueryManyIter` using cached stable/component locations. This keeps Zircon aligned with Bevy's `QueryState::iter_many_manual(...)` motivation of reusing already-updated query state, while intentionally diverging by exposing a named cached-direct path that can prove table/sparse location reads before default query iteration changes.

The cached entity-index slice removes the remaining repeated linear scans from cached targeted membership checks. `QueryState::update_cache(world)` now keeps `cached_entities` in scene order for iteration and also builds a sorted stable-entity-to-cache-row index for cached `get`, `contains`, cached `iter_many`, cached-direct `iter_many`, and mutable validation. This preserves caller order and duplicate read-only entity semantics while making each cached membership lookup binary-search the index instead of scanning the whole cached candidate list. The direction is Bevy's targeted query model: Bevy documents `QueryState::get_manual(...)` as `O(1)` in `dev/bevy/crates/bevy_ecs/src/query/state.rs`, and Bevy's many/query iterators use entity location plus table row fetches in `dev/bevy/crates/bevy_ecs/src/query/iter.rs`; Zircon is not yet at that O(1) entity table but now has the explicit cache-row indirection needed for the next dense fetch step.

The default read-only cached-location slice makes the normal system `Query::iter(...)` path consume those cached locations. `QueryIter` still has the same public role and item type, but cached construction now carries `StableEntityLocation` plus per-row `ComponentStorageLocation` slices. `QueryData::fetch_with_component_locations(...)` and `QueryFilter::matches_component_locations(...)` are the location-aware hooks used by that iterator; built-in `&T`, read-only `&mut T`, `Ref<T>`, read-only `Mut<T>`, optional reads, `EntityId`, `StableEntityLocation`, tuple data, `With<T>`, `Without<T>`, `Added<T>`, and `Changed<T>` all implement the cached-location path. This mirrors the Bevy iterator shape in `dev/bevy/crates/bevy_ecs/src/query/iter.rs`, where the cursor sets table/archetype context and then fetches by table row, while preserving Zircon's existing `QueryIter` API and stable scene-id ordering. Manual non-cached `QueryState::iter(world)` still scans `World::entity_ids_for_query()` and fetches normally; cached/manual `iter_cached(...)` and default system `Query::iter(...)` now use the cached component-location path.

The M11 performance acceptance slice adds deterministic hot-path counters before treating this area as speed-complete. Bevy's own query fast path is grounded in updated `QueryState` metadata plus table/archetype cursor fetches in `dev/bevy/crates/bevy_ecs/src/query/state.rs`, `iter.rs`, and `fetch.rs`; Zircon's local acceptance therefore asserts cache behavior directly instead of enforcing brittle wall-clock thresholds. The `ecs_performance_acceptance` tests record elapsed microseconds for spawn/query/change/transform smoke data, but the pass criteria are structural: repeated system queries must keep the same cached candidate set, value replacement must not rebuild that cache, a matching spawn must rebuild exactly once, `Changed<T>` must report only the mutated entity subset without rebuilding, and repeated transform reads must project stable world transforms while leaving dirty-state flushing to the scheduled internal systems.

The unique read-only `iter_many` slice adds `UniqueEntityArray<N>`, `QueryState::iter_many_unique(...)`, `QueryState::iter_many_unique_cached(...)`, `QueryState::iter_many_unique_cached_direct(...)`, and the matching system `Query` helpers. This follows Bevy's `Query::iter_many_unique(...)`, `QueryState::iter_many_unique(...)`, `QueryManyUniqueIter`, and `EntitySet` design in `dev/bevy/crates/bevy_ecs/src/system/query.rs`, `dev/bevy/crates/bevy_ecs/src/query/state.rs`, `dev/bevy/crates/bevy_ecs/src/query/iter.rs`, and `dev/bevy/crates/bevy_ecs/src/entity/entity_set.rs`: the entity list must be proven unique up front, results are yielded in caller order, and missing or non-matching entities are skipped. Zircon deliberately implements only the fixed-size `UniqueEntityArray<N>` wrapper for now, not Bevy's full trait family; this gives query callers a typed uniqueness precondition while reusing the existing `QueryManyIter` / `CachedQueryManyIter` fetch paths for stable scene `EntityId` values.

The mutable targeted-get slice adds `QueryState::get_mut(...)`, `QueryState::get_many_mut(...)`, `Query::get_mut(...)`, and `Query::get_many_mut(...)` for the current single-item `QueryMutData` surface. This follows Bevy's `Query::get_mut` and `Query::get_many_mut_inner` in `dev/bevy/crates/bevy_ecs/src/system/query.rs`, plus `QueryState::get_mut` / `get_many_mut` in `dev/bevy/crates/bevy_ecs/src/query/state.rs`. Zircon rejects duplicate stable entity ids before fetching mutable items and returns `QueryEntityError::AliasedMutability(entity)`, matching Bevy's aliasing diagnostic intent while staying narrower than Bevy's full `IterQueryData` surface. The focused regression tests extract expected `Err` values through an explicit helper instead of `unwrap_err()` because the `Ok` variant carries mutable world/query borrows; keeping that drop boundary explicit avoids compiler diagnostics obscuring the actual alias/mismatch assertions during profiling-profile lib-test builds.

The mutable `iter_many_mut` slice adds `QueryManyMutIter`, `QueryState::iter_many_mut(...)`, and system `Query::iter_many_mut(...)`. It follows Bevy's `Query::iter_many_mut` and `QueryManyIter::fetch_next()` contract in `dev/bevy/crates/bevy_ecs/src/system/query.rs` and `dev/bevy/crates/bevy_ecs/src/query/iter.rs`: requested entities are visited in caller order, missing or non-matching entities are skipped, and duplicate requested entity IDs are allowed only through a cursor-style `fetch_next()` API that ties the returned mutable item to the iterator borrow. Zircon deliberately does not implement `Iterator` for `QueryManyMutIter`; this keeps the aliasing boundary explicit while the local mutable query surface remains single-item `QueryMutData`. The iterator refreshes and clones the `QueryState` structural candidate list at creation, then rechecks required data and dynamic filters against the active tick window before each mutable fetch.

The full mutable `iter_mut` slice adds `QueryMutIter`, `QueryState::iter_mut(...)`, and system `Query::iter_mut(...)`. It follows Bevy's full-query mutable iterator surface in `dev/bevy/crates/bevy_ecs/src/query/state.rs` and `dev/bevy/crates/bevy_ecs/src/system/query.rs`, while staying on Zircon's narrower single-item `QueryMutData` contract. The iterator refreshes `QueryState`, snapshots the cached stable entity ids, and yields ordinary `Iterator` items because the cache contains each live structural candidate once. Dynamic filters such as `Changed<T>` are still checked at fetch time against the active system tick window, so the iterator does not bake run-window membership into the structural cache.

The mutable-data read-only projection slice implements the local subset of Bevy's `D::ReadOnly` behavior without adding a full query-lens/type-family system yet. Bevy documents that immutable `Query` methods return `ROQueryItem` and substitute mutable fetch references with shared references in `dev/bevy/crates/bevy_ecs/src/system/query.rs`, and its `as_readonly` / `into_readonly` paths convert `Query<(&mut D1, &D2), F>` into a read-only data shape before `iter()` delegates through that read-only query. Bevy's `QueryState::iter` similarly returns `D::ReadOnly` in `dev/bevy/crates/bevy_ecs/src/query/state.rs`. Zircon now models the observable helper behavior directly on the existing traits: `QueryData for &mut T` yields `&T`, `QueryData for Mut<T>` yields `Ref<T>`, and matching `CachedQueryData` implementations make the same projection available to `iter_cached_direct(...)`, `get_cached_direct(...)`, and system cached-direct helpers. The access descriptor intentionally remains write-classified because the original query marker is still mutable; this preserves Zircon's conservative schedule-conflict behavior until a dedicated read-only query lens can clear writes the way Bevy does internally.

The unique mutable `iter_many` slice adds `QueryManyUniqueMutIter`, `QueryState::iter_many_unique_mut(...)`, and system `Query::iter_many_unique_mut(...)` over `UniqueEntityArray<N>`. This is the mutable counterpart to Bevy's `Query::iter_many_unique_mut(...)` / `QueryManyUniqueIter` path in `dev/bevy/crates/bevy_ecs/src/system/query.rs` and `dev/bevy/crates/bevy_ecs/src/query/iter.rs`: once the entity input is proven unique, the iterator may yield ordinary `Iterator` items because each mutable component item comes from a distinct stable entity id. Zircon keeps the existing duplicate-tolerant `QueryManyMutIter::fetch_next()` path for general entity lists and uses the unique wrapper only for the Bevy-style iterator surface.

The unique mutable many-get slice adds `QueryState::get_many_unique_mut(...)` and system `Query::get_many_unique_mut(...)` over `UniqueEntityArray<N>`. This follows Bevy's `QueryState::get_many_unique_mut(...)` in `dev/bevy/crates/bevy_ecs/src/query/state.rs`, system `Query::get_many_unique_mut(...)` in `dev/bevy/crates/bevy_ecs/src/system/query.rs`, and the unique entity input contract in `dev/bevy/crates/bevy_ecs/src/entity/entity_set.rs`. Zircon reuses the existing mutable many-get validation path after the unique wrapper is consumed, so every returned mutable item is still validated against liveness, cached structural candidates, required data, and the active `Added<T>` / `Changed<T>` run window before any mutable fetch occurs.

The combination slices add read-only `QueryCombinationIter`, mutable `QueryCombinationMutIter`, `QueryState::iter_combinations::<K>(...)`, `QueryState::iter_combinations_cached::<K>(...)`, `QueryState::iter_combinations_mut::<K>(...)`, and matching system `Query` helpers. This follows Bevy's `QueryCombinationIter` / `fetch_next()` design in `dev/bevy/crates/bevy_ecs/src/query/iter.rs`, `QueryState::iter_combinations` / `iter_combinations_mut` in `dev/bevy/crates/bevy_ecs/src/query/state.rs`, and `Query::iter_combinations` / `iter_combinations_mut` in `dev/bevy/crates/bevy_ecs/src/system/query.rs`: `K == N` yields one group, `K < N` yields each unique group without repetition, `K > N` yields no groups, and `K == 0` is rejected. Zircon's read-only iterator implements `Iterator`; the mutable cursor deliberately exposes only `fetch_next()` so returned mutable items are tied to the iterator's `&mut self` borrow and cannot be held across another fetch. Both paths preserve the stable scene entity order of the candidate list, refresh cached structural candidates for cached or mutable variants, and recheck `Added<T>` / `Changed<T>` filters against the active tick window before the combination list is formed.

System `Query::iter_cached(...)` / `Query::single_cached(...)` provide the named non-direct cached path for normal read-only `QueryData` over the active system tick window. This mirrors Bevy's `QueryState::iter(&mut self, world)` cache-refresh contract in `dev/bevy/crates/bevy_ecs/src/query/state.rs`. Default system `Query::iter()` now follows the same persistent-cache candidate path through the run item's exclusive `SystemState` borrow, while the explicit cached methods remain useful for call sites that want the cache choice visible in code review and tests.

The count/empty/contains helper slices add `QueryState::count(...)`, `QueryState::is_empty(...)`, `QueryState::contains(...)`, cached normal variants, cached-direct variants, and matching system `Query` methods. This follows Bevy's system `Query::is_empty` / `Query::count` / `Query::contains` convenience API in `dev/bevy/crates/bevy_ecs/src/system/query.rs` and Bevy's tick-aware `QueryState::is_empty(world, last_run, this_run)` / `QueryState::contains(entity, world, last_run, this_run)` shape in `dev/bevy/crates/bevy_ecs/src/query/state.rs`. Zircon's cached helper variants deliberately keep the cache choice explicit for manual `QueryState` callers: non-cached helpers use the normal query predicates, while `*_cached` and `*_cached_direct` refresh and reuse the persistent candidate list before still rechecking run-window filters such as `Changed<T>`. System `Query::iter(...)`, `Query::single(...)`, `Query::count(...)`, `Query::is_empty(...)`, and read-only `Query::iter_combinations(...)` now also refresh and consume the persistent structural candidate cache by default, matching Bevy's system-query model more closely without changing targeted `get(...)` semantics. `contains(...)` first rejects missing scene entities, so optional-data queries such as `(EntityId, Option<&T>)` cannot accidentally match stale or nonexistent ids.

This is still a stepping stone, not the final M11 hot path. The runtime records table and sparse-set signatures plus cached stable/component locations, default system read-only iteration avoids full-world scans and fetches through cached component locations, explicit cached-direct iteration can read table/sparse rows for read-only queries, cached targeted membership now uses a cache-row index, and mutable iteration already consumes cached structural candidates. The next performance slice can add benchmark acceptance data for spawn/query/change/transform hot paths.

## System Params, Commands, And Change Detection

`SystemState<P>` is the local system execution wrapper. It caches `P::State`, records `SystemParamAccess`, tracks the system's `last_run` tick, advances `World` to a fresh `ChangeTick` for each run, and fetches `P::Item<'_>` for the user closure. After the closure returns and the previous active change tick is restored, it calls `P::record_performance_diagnostics(...)` so persistent params can publish frame-level counters without changing the user-facing `Query` item API. Tuple params compose up to eight items and share one access descriptor so duplicate mutable resource, component, or event access is rejected before a system runs. A crate-private state accessor exists for regression tests that need to verify persistent query cache rebuild counts without exposing that state outside `zircon_runtime`.

Supported system params in this slice are:

- `QueryState<D, F>` as a param, yielding `Query<D, F>` with `iter()`, `iter_many(entities)`, `iter_many_unique(entities)`, `iter_combinations::<K>()`, `single()`, `get(entity)`, `get_many([entities])`, `get_many_unique(entities)`, `count()`, `is_empty()`, `contains(entity)`, explicit read-only `iter_cached()` / `iter_many_cached(entities)` / `iter_many_unique_cached(entities)` / `iter_combinations_cached::<K>()` / `single_cached()` / `get_cached(entity)` / `get_many_cached([entities])` / `get_many_unique_cached(entities)` / `count_cached()` / `is_empty_cached()` / `contains_cached(entity)`, direct read-only `iter_cached_direct()` / `iter_many_cached_direct(entities)` / `iter_many_unique_cached_direct(entities)` / `single_cached_direct()` / `get_cached_direct(entity)` / `get_many_cached_direct([entities])` / `get_many_unique_cached_direct(entities)` / `count_cached_direct()` / `is_empty_cached_direct()` / `contains_cached_direct(entity)`, and mutable `iter_mut()` / `single_mut()` / `get_mut(entity)` / `get_many_mut([entities])` / `get_many_unique_mut(entities)` / `iter_many_mut(entities)` / `iter_many_unique_mut(entities)` / `iter_combinations_mut::<K>()` / cache-backed `for_each_mut(...)` over the current tick window. The cached normal, cached direct, and mutable system-query paths reuse the persistent `QueryState` stored inside `SystemState`, mirroring Bevy's separation between query state and each run's iterator cursor.
- `ResParam<T>` and `ResMutParam<T>`, yielding `Res<T>` and `ResMut<T>` wrappers with `Deref`, `DerefMut` for mutable access, and `is_added()` / `is_changed()` / `last_changed()` helpers.
- `Option<ResParam<T>>` and `Option<ResMutParam<T>>`, returning `None` when a resource is absent while still recording the declared access. Required `ResParam<T>` and `ResMutParam<T>` fail `SystemState::new(...)` with `SystemParamError::MissingResource` if the resource is missing.
- `CommandsParam`, yielding `Commands`, which queues deferred `spawn`, `spawn_empty`, `entity`, `entity_or_spawn`, `despawn`, component insert/remove, bundle insert, resource insert/remove, or custom `Command`/closure work.
- `EventReaderParam<T>` and `EventWriterParam<T>`, yielding cursor-based current-queue readers (`iter`, `unread_count`, `clear`) and single or counted batch next-queue writers over runtime `EventStore`.
- `MessageReaderParam<T>` and `MessageWriterParam<T>`, yielding cursor-based scheduled message reads and immediate single or batch writes to runtime `MessageStore`.
- `RemovedComponentsParam<T>`, yielding a stateful `RemovedComponents<T>` reader that reports entities whose `T` was removed after the reader's last cursor.
- `ParamSet<(P0, P1, ...)>` for arities one through eight, allowing potentially conflicting params to be declared together while exposing only segmented `p0()` through `p7()` access.
- `LocalParam<T>`, yielding persistent local state initialized from `T::default()` and preserved across `SystemState::run(...)` calls.

`ParamSet` checks each child parameter against the access baseline from outside the set, then merges child access into the outer descriptor without preserving sibling query filters. This keeps sibling params allowed to conflict with each other while making the aggregate access conservative when another system compares against the whole `ParamSet`. Runtime 07 also requires `ParamSet` to forward `record_performance_diagnostics(...)` into every child state, so `QueryState` telemetry is not lost when a system accesses it through `p0()` through `p7()`.

`SystemParamAccess::conflicts_with(...)` is the M5 boolean compatibility path used by scheduler graph construction. It checks `QueryAccess::conflicts_with(...)`, resource read/write intersections, event read/write intersections, and message read/write intersections directly, so non-conflicting candidate pairs do not allocate a detailed conflict vector. Resource IDs and event/message `TypeId` lists are inserted at their binary-search position, giving both the boolean path and the detailed diagnostic path sorted membership checks without push-and-sort churn. `SystemParamAccess::conflict_kinds_with(...)` remains the M11 bridge from system parameters into schedule diagnostics. It reports component conflicts from `QueryAccess`, resource conflicts by `ResourceId`, and event/message conflicts by `TypeId` whenever two access descriptors share data and at least one side writes. Deferred commands are not treated as data conflicts in this graph; native systems whose access declares `CommandsParam` contribute a separate `ScheduledSceneStep::ApplyDeferred` sync step immediately after that native system in the sorted stage plan. The native-system conflict graph mirrors that schedule shape with a barrier node so diagnostics and later parallel planning see the synchronization boundary without inventing a false data conflict.

`World::commands()` exposes the same deferred queue outside system params, and `World::apply_deferred()` applies queued commands at a fresh active change tick. Command effects are intentionally invisible until `apply_deferred()` runs, which gives schedule work a deterministic sync point equivalent to Bevy's `ApplyDeferred`. Runtime 08-M2.2 makes that sync point observable: `apply_deferred()` returns `DeferredCommandReport` with the applied command count plus ordered `DeferredCommandError` entries. `Commands::insert`, `insert_bundle`, `remove`, `despawn`, and the matching `EntityCommands` helpers report missing or already-despawned entity targets with operation, entity id, and the world-layer error message instead of dropping those failures silently. `Commands::spawn(...)` and `spawn_empty()` reserve stable `EntityId` values from `World::next_id` before application, so `EntityCommands::id()` is stable even while the entity is not yet visible in the world. Runtime 08's command facade owner is folder-backed under `scene/ecs/commands/commands/`: `facade.rs` owns `Commands`, `entity_commands.rs` owns `EntityCommands`, and `param.rs` owns `CommandsParam`. `CommandQueue` now keeps ordinary ordered `Command` payloads in 64-byte-aligned, 192-byte inline slots under a 4 MiB active slot-storage budget. Oversized, over-aligned, or budget-exhausted payloads use an explicit boxed fallback; `CommandQueueMetrics` separates logical payload bytes from bounded occupied slot bytes, and exposes backing storage growth, explicit fallback allocation/release, inline release, dispatch calls, and panic-discard counts. `FnCommand` keeps closure commands available. An unwind drops every unconsumed payload, restores the active change tick, and preserves commands enqueued during application for the next deferred apply. `WorkerCommandBuffer` owns a prewarmable local queue per external command callback. A worker-safe callback batch runs without the World lock, then `SceneScheduleRunner` sorts buffer keys by `(system_order, system_id)`, rejects duplicate keys before transfer, merges once into the World queue, and applies the merged batch once. Main-thread-only or constrained callbacks retain an immediate ApplyDeferred barrier. Typed `CommandsParam` systems do not yet use this worker callback contract because their entity-reservation and World-parameter semantics remain explicit.

Change detection uses a `u64`-backed, wrap-aware `ChangeTick`. `ChangeTick::next()` wraps at the integer boundary, `ChangeTick::relative_to(...)` uses wrapping subtraction, and `ChangeTick::is_newer_than(...)` compares clamped relative ages from the current `this_run` tick. `ChangeTickWindow::new(...)` clamps a stale `last_run` baseline to `ChangeTick::MAX_CHANGE_AGE`, so a long-idle system window does not turn old component ticks into false positives after counter wrap. Zircon intentionally keeps this deterministic window/compare clamp instead of adding Bevy's periodic storage-wide tick scan in this slice. Direct world mutations outside a system advance the world tick immediately. Mutations inside `SystemState::run(...)` use that system's active tick, so the mutating system does not see its own previous run as changed on the next run while other systems with older `last_run` windows can observe the change. `Changed<T>` intentionally includes newly added components, matching Bevy. `World::get_mut`, `World::insert`, resource mutation, deferred command application, fixed scene component maps, and typed ECS storage all flow through this tick path.

Removed-component events are runtime-only world state. Direct `World::remove<T>`, recursive/despawn entity removal, deferred `EntityCommands::remove<T>()`, and deferred despawn all record the removed component type and stable entity id. The reader cursor lives in `RemovedComponentReader<T>` / `RemovedComponentsParam<T>`, so each system observes only events it has not already consumed.

## Schedule Model

`Schedule` owns a `SceneSystemRegistry` and consumes the public stage order whose unique declaration owner is `core/framework/scene/system_stage.rs`. The fixed stage order is:

`First -> PreUpdate -> FixedFirst -> FixedUpdate -> FixedPostUpdate -> Update -> PostUpdate -> Last -> RenderExtract`.

`LateUpdate` is intentionally absent. This is a hard cutover to the Bevy-style `PostUpdate` name plus Zircon's explicit `RenderExtract` stage.

`SceneSystemDescriptor` records a stable system id, target stage, deterministic order, and the internal built-in system enum. `SceneSystemRegistry` rejects blank or whitespace-padded ids, rejects duplicate ids, and maintains descriptor/native-system lists in stage-rank, order, and id order through binary-position insertion when systems are registered or restored. The built-in registry currently installs:

- `zircon.scene.hierarchy_validity` in `PostUpdate`;
- `zircon.scene.active_hierarchy` in `PostUpdate`;
- `zircon.scene.world_transform` in `PostUpdate`;
- `zircon.scene.node_cache` in `PostUpdate`;
- `zircon.scene.render_extract_prepare` in `RenderExtract`.

`InternalSceneSystem::ApplyDeferred` is a runtime-only synchronization system that calls `World::apply_deferred()`. It can be registered explicitly when a stage needs a named sync point. Native systems whose `SystemParamAccess` declares `CommandsParam` now expose the same boundary as an explicit `ScheduledSceneStep::ApplyDeferred` immediately after the producing native system, so diagnostics and later parallel planning can see the sync point instead of relying on a hidden side effect inside `World::run_native_scene_system(...)`. The runner still flushes deferred commands after every internal system except `ApplyDeferred` itself and after every plugin hook, preserving existing hook and built-in semantics. This is a deterministic local integration point, not Bevy's full graph analysis.

Native scene system objects live under `scene::ecs::system::native` and are part of the curated `zircon_runtime::scene::ecs` root surface. The root module re-exports `BoxedSceneSystem`, `FunctionSceneSystem`, `IntoSceneSystem`, `SceneSystem`, and `SceneSystemMetadata` beside `SystemParam` so registry, schedule, and world registration code can depend on the stable ECS surface instead of importing the private native subtree. The native scheduled-step cache remains crate-private and only stores native system ids plus generated `ApplyDeferred` sync steps. Internal systems and plugin hooks stay borrowed through `ScheduledSceneStepRef<'_>` while `ScheduledSceneStep::iter_sorted_for_stage(...)` merges the already stage-specific slices by `order`, `id`, and step rank, so a command-producing native system runs before its generated sync step at the same order/id without materializing owned internal or hook steps.

`ScheduleConflictGraph` is the first local graph-analysis primitive for M11. Callers provide `ScheduleConflictNode` values containing a system id, `SystemStage`, `SystemParamAccess`, and a node kind. Normal system nodes compare only against other systems in the same stage, first using the allocation-free `SystemParamAccess::conflicts_with(...)` boolean gate and then materializing concrete `SystemParamConflictKind` entries only for pairs that become edges. Barrier nodes represent explicit sync work such as generated native `ApplyDeferred`; they stay visible in node order but do not emit conflict edges because they are ordering boundaries rather than access descriptors. This follows Bevy's schedule-build separation between system access metadata and ambiguity/conflicting-system diagnostics, but it is currently diagnostic-only.

`ScheduleConflictGraph::conservative_parallel_batches()` produces the first batch plan for later `JobScheduler` integration. It walks nodes in deterministic input order, groups adjacent same-stage systems into a `ScheduleParallelBatch` only when the graph-owned node-index adjacency cache has no conflict between the new node and the sorted node-index list already in the last batch, and starts a new batch at every conflict, stage boundary, or barrier node. Barrier batches expose `ScheduleParallelBatch::has_barrier()` and carry the barrier id, such as `apply_deferred:<system_id>`, so future runners can execute sync work sequentially between producer and consumer batches. This is intentionally conservative: it preserves existing order semantics and avoids moving a later non-conflicting system ahead of an intervening conflicting system while avoiding repeated edge-list scans and repeated system-id hash lookups during batch construction. Public `systems_conflict(...)` resolves system ids through a node-index map and reuses the same sorted adjacency vectors instead of maintaining a second string-keyed adjacency cache. `conservative_parallel_batches()` uses `node_indices_conflict_with_any(...)` to intersect the active batch's sorted node indices with the candidate node's sorted adjacency list, so one candidate no longer performs one binary lookup for every node already in the batch. `conflicts_for(...)` resolves the requested id through that same map and follows node-owned edge-index vectors into the stable edge record slice, so graph construction no longer clones system ids into a separate diagnostic edge-index map. `ScheduleConflictGraph::from_nodes(...)` keeps global left-node traversal order, advances a per-stage cursor for the current left node, and gets right-side candidates from per-stage node-index buckets, so construction only compares later nodes from the same stage instead of walking every later graph node, rejecting stage mismatches one at a time, or searching the left node inside its bucket on every iteration. `ScheduleParallelBatch` still exposes public system ids for diagnostics and executor task lookup; its private node indices are only the graph-owned hot-path representation.

`ScheduleParallelExecutor` is the first M11 `JobScheduler` execution bridge. It consumes `ScheduleParallelBatch` values and a `ScheduleParallelTaskRegistry`, verifies that every system id in a batch has a registered task before starting that batch, runs single-task batches inline through one direct registry lookup, runs two-task batches through a fixed `rayon::join(...)` pair path, runs three-task batches through a nested fixed `rayon::join(...)` path, and reserves the generic Rayon iterator path for four-or-more batches. Batch boundaries remain sequential: all tasks in one batch finish before the next batch starts, and task failures are reported in deterministic batch order through `ScheduleParallelExecutorError`. The four-or-more preflight path reserves an exact-capacity task-reference vector from the batch system-id count, then pushes each validated task in batch order before scheduling. The four-or-more success path collects task results into an ordered `Vec` without an executor-owned result mutex, completion-order sorting, or success-path system-id clones; it recovers and clones the failed system id from the batch only when a task returns an error. This executor is deliberately limited to independent registered closures; it does not yet run mutable `World` systems or plugin hooks in parallel.

`SceneScheduleRunner` merges built-in descriptors, native system ids, generated native `ApplyDeferred` sync steps, and scene-owned `SceneRuntimeHookRegistration` values for each stage. `Schedule` owns an executor-facing `Arc<SceneScheduleStagePlan>` that is rebuilt when internal or native systems are registered and when a schedule is deserialized. `WorldDriver` owns installed scene hooks as a canonical flat order plus an `Arc<SceneRuntimeHookStagePlan>` rebuilt only when hooks are installed, and clones both cached plans once per tick. Core runtime retains only data-only devtools hook snapshots; it no longer stores executable hooks or imports the plugin/scene hook contract. The driver passes only the current stage's hook slice into each stage runner, so the sorted stage plan no longer scans all hooks or clones every stage bucket to rediscover the same stage. Native system execution temporarily removes the boxed system from `World`'s schedule registry, runs it with `&mut World`, and restores it without rebuilding the executor plan per system; any native command flush happens only when the separate scheduled sync step executes. Hooks run outside the world mutex, so a hook can safely call `LevelSystem::with_world_mut(...)` while still participating in the same `order`/`id` sorted stage sequence. The runner defers eager derived-state flushing while a stage is running so hooks can mutate local scene state through the normal dirty path. When the stage finishes successfully, the runner disables defer mode and runs that stage's built-in systems once more.

Plugins01 adds a narrower production parallel path without changing the generic `ScheduleParallelExecutor` contract above. Registration-manifest systems default to main-thread-only conservative world access. A system enters a worker batch only when its stable component/resource access has been resolved into `SystemParamAccess`, the host granted its worker-safe capability, its callback supports worldless execution, and it has no explicit ordering constraint. `SceneScheduleStagePlan` caches the native conflict graph; `SceneScheduleRunner` greedily batches only adjacent worker-safe systems and flushes at conflicts, main-thread systems, runtime systems, hooks, internal systems, and deferred barriers. Before dispatch it removes the boxed systems under the world lock, releases that lock, runs the worldless callbacks through `JobScheduler::join(...)`, and restores every system before propagating a callback panic. No `World` pointer, Rust trait object, or unchecked DLL function pointer crosses the plugin ABI. Legacy direct ABI-v3 systems and empty/`write:world` registration paths remain main-thread conservative writers.

Runtime 07 M0.3 also makes `SceneScheduleRunner::run_stage(...)` the owner of the stage-level frame profiling span. Each stage entry records `runtime_frame_schedule_stage.<SystemStage>` through `profile_dynamic_scope!("runtime", "frame", ...)`, which keeps profiling overhead compiled out unless profiling features are enabled and does not alter the borrowed sorted-step iterator, deferred-flush mode, hook execution, or final consistency sweep semantics. The `runtime_07_hotspot_inventory_requires_counted_evidence_before_m2` guard keeps this source anchor synchronized with the Runtime 07 plan, hotspot inventory, dynamic session frame-span documentation, and runtime index.

Runtime 07 M1.1 adds `EcsFramePerformanceDiagnostics` as the ECS-side frame aggregation object. It stays beside the ECS query/change-detection owners and merges multiple `QueryStateCacheStats` plus `ChangeDetectionScanStats` samples into one frame-indexed `DiagnosticStore` write. Query cached revision uses the newest revision seen across samples, while count fields use saturating addition. Extract rebuild/output-byte diagnostics are not part of this object; they remain dynamic-session owned because `RuntimeDynamicSession::current_extract()` is the production extract construction point.

`World` stores the current frame aggregate as skipped serialized state, exposes `reset_ecs_frame_performance_diagnostics()` and `ecs_frame_performance_diagnostics()`, and accepts QueryState samples through `record_ecs_query_cache_stats(...)` plus `record_ecs_change_detection_stats(...)`. Runtime 15 F14 removed the old `WorldEcsFramePerformanceDiagnostics` pure wrapper: the field now directly holds `EcsFramePerformanceDiagnostics`, and `scene/world/performance_diagnostics.rs` calls the ECS diagnostics object without `.0` forwarding. `EcsFramePerformanceDiagnostics` also implements `FrameDiagnostics` with the `scene.ecs` domain so ECS frame counters use the same domain/availability contract as core runtime diagnostics. `WorldDriver::tick_level(...)` resets that aggregate before the frame's stage loop and calls `EcsFramePerformanceDiagnostics::publish(...)` after the completed loop. Repeated `SystemState::run(...)` calls therefore accumulate inside one tick, every completed tick publishes the fixed query/change counter set plus native-system `conflict_count`, average `ready_delay_ms`, `worker_utilization`, `callback_p95_ms`, worker-batch count, callback count, and `conservative_world_writer_count` into the runtime diagnostic store (including zero-valued idle frames), and the next tick starts with an empty frame snapshot. The conservative-writer counter is frozen into each cached native schedule step from its compiled `SystemParamAccess`, so it observes legacy fallback without reparsing manifest strings on the frame path. Native callback p95 uses a fixed 65-bucket nanosecond histogram, while worker utilization divides summed callback busy time by elapsed batch capacity at the active scheduler width. Publication iterates fixed-size counter arrays owned by the query/change/native schedule statistics modules and does not allocate a temporary diagnostic snapshot on the frame hot path.

`ecs_frame_performance_diagnostics_record_query_and_change_counts` is the source-level acceptance anchor for the aggregation contract: it records two query samples and two change-detection samples, then verifies that `ecs.query.*` and `ecs.change_detection.*` diagnostic paths expose the combined frame values. `system_state_records_query_cache_stats_into_world_frame_diagnostics` is the automatic collection anchor: two `SystemState<QueryState<...>>` runs in one frame produce two query cache hits in the world frame aggregate, then a frame reset causes the next single run to report only one additional hit. `headless_session_tick_publishes_ecs_frame_diagnostics` is the end-to-end publication anchor: a real headless dynamic-session tick must make the query-cache and change-scan series visible through `collect_runtime_diagnostics(...)`, even when that frame's current values are zero.

`system_state_records_change_detection_stats_into_world_frame_diagnostics` is the change-detection collection anchor. Cached QueryState reads call `QueryFilter::matches_component_locations_with_stats(...)` for `Added<T>` and `Changed<T>`, iterator drop paths and cached read helpers merge `ChangeDetectionScanStats` back into `QueryState`, and `SystemParam::record_performance_diagnostics(...)` drains `take_unreported_change_detection_stats()` into the world frame aggregate after the system run. Cached iterators keep their diagnostics sink as `NonNull<QueryState<D, F>>`, so the cached slice borrow still governs validity while read-only, non-cached iterators used by `QueryState::single` do not gain a spurious state-reference lifetime. This keeps scanned mark and changed/added match counts tied to real system execution instead of requiring manual diagnostics writes from callers.
